Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore—not my art classes, board-game nights, hiking, or even getting back to the soccer team, and I just sit in my room while everything slips away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m here because… I don’t know, I just don’t care about anything anymore. Like, things I used to actually look forward to don’t feel like anything now. My art classes used to matter to me a lot, and I’d go even when I was tired because it felt like mine, you know? And board-game nights with people, hiking, even thinking about getting back to the soccer team… I used to have these little threads pulling me out of the house.

Now I mostly just sit in my room and let everything slip away. I know I’m missing things, and I know I’m supposed to want to fix it or at least be upset enough to do something, but it’s like there’s no spark there. I’m just kind of watching my life shrink, and I can’t make myself care the way I used to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels like the pleasure is pretty much gone. I keep trying to check, like maybe there’s still a little bit there and I’m just not noticing it, but… no. My art classes used to feel important to me, like even if I was having a rough week, I’d still get something out of showing up and making something. Now I just think about going and it feels flat, like a chore I don’t even have the energy to pretend to care about.

Same with board-game nights. I used to like the planning, the jokes, the whole routine of it. Hiking too—I used to feel clearer after being outside, even just a little. And soccer, I kept telling myself maybe I’d get back into it, but even that idea doesn’t pull at me anymore. I mostly just end up sitting in my room, letting those things pass by, and I know they used to matter, but I can’t feel that they matter now. It’s like the part of me that enjoyed them just… shut off.

this.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: For much of this past year, the low mood and the voices have been happening at the same time; I would be lying in bed feeling hopeless while also hearing that woman telling me I ruined everything.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: I know I’m missing things, and I know I’m supposed to want to fix it or at least be upset enough to do something, but it’s like there’s no spark there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.

Most of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.

So if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. Maybe little blips where I was distracted for a few hours, but nothing that felt like I was back.

Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really. I mean, I can put a movie on, or open a book, but it’s like I’m just… occupying my eyes. I don’t feel pulled into it the way I used to. I’ll sit there and realize I’ve watched twenty minutes and I couldn’t even tell you what happened, and it doesn’t bother me enough to rewind. I just let it keep going because at least the room isn’t completely silent.

It’s the same feeling as with everything else. I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not board-game nights, not hiking, not even the idea of getting back to the soccer team. Those things used to be parts of my life, like proof that I had interests and people and reasons to leave the house. Now I mostly just sit in my room while everything slips away, and even when I try to do something by myself, there’s no pleasure in it. It’s just time passing.

the pleasure completely gone from activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: It feels like the pleasure is pretty much gone. I keep trying to check, like maybe there’s still a little bit there and I’m just not noticing it, but… no. My art classes used to feel important to me, like even if I was having a rough week, I’d still get something out of showing up and making something. Now I just think about going and it feels flat, like a chore I don’t even have the energy to pretend to care about.\n\nSame with board-game nights. I used to like the planning, the jokes, the whole routine of it. Hiking too—I used to feel clearer after being outside, even just a little. I mostly just end up sitting in my room, letting those things pass by, and I know they used to matter, but I can’t feel that they matter now. I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.\n\nMost of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. Maybe little blips where I was distracted for a few hours, but nothing that felt like I was back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you're doing something by yourself, like reading or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can put a movie on, or open a book, but it’s like I’m just… occupying my eyes. I don’t feel pulled into it the way I used to. I’ll sit there and realize I’ve watched twenty minutes and I couldn’t even tell you what happened, and it doesn’t bother me enough to rewind. I just let it keep going because at least the room isn’t completely silent.\n\nIt’s the same feeling as with everything else. I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not board-game nights, not hiking, not even the idea of getting back to the soccer team. Those things used to be parts of my life, like proof that I had interests and people and reasons to leave the house. Now I mostly just sit in my room while everything slips away, and even when I try to do something by myself, there’s no pleasure in it. Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.

It’s not always like I’m making some clear plan in my head, but I keep having these thoughts like… I just don’t want to wake up anymore. I remember feeling embarrassed afterward, because it felt dramatic or like I was scaring people, but in the moment it didn’t feel dramatic. It felt true. Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you're doing something by I mean, I can put a movie on, or open a book, but it’s like I’m just… occupying my eyes. I don’t feel pulled into it the way I used to. I’ll sit there and realize I’ve watched twenty minutes and I couldn’t even tell you what happened, and it doesn’t bother me enough to rewind. I haven’t really been tracking it as a separate thing.\n\nSometimes I feel uneasy, but I’m not always sure if that’s worry or just me feeling bad in general. It’s hard to separate it out. If you mean do I sit there with a clear list of things I’m worried about and feel nervous about them all day, I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. But if you mean this uncomfortable, unsettled feeling that’s just there sometimes, then maybe. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive during this time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m making some clear plan in my head, but I keep having these thoughts like… I just don’t want to wake up anymore. Like if I could go to sleep and just not have to come back into this feeling, that would be a relief. It sounds awful when I say it plainly, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nAnd there was a time my friend found me, and I guess I had been saying it out loud — that I couldn’t keep living like this. I remember feeling embarrassed afterward, because it felt dramatic or like I was scaring people, but in the moment it didn’t feel dramatic. It felt true. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. I don’t know the exact number because I haven’t really been weighing myself regularly, but I can tell it’s changed. My jeans are really loose now — like not just “a little baggy,” but loose enough that I’ve had to knot the waistband to keep them from sliding around. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll notice it when I’m getting dressed and then just… not do anything about it.

And eating has been strange. Most days I’ll go almost the whole day without eating, not because I’m trying to lose weight or anything like that, but because food just doesn’t have any appeal. It tastes like cardboard, or like I’m chewing just to get through it. I’ll think, “I should probably eat something,” and then hours go by and I still haven’t. So yeah, I think I’ve lost weight, probably a significant amount, but I don’t have a clean number to give you.

It started not long after my divorce, like shortly after everything was finalized and I was trying to pretend I could just restart my life. At first I thought it was just the normal fallout, you know, like of course I’d feel empty for a while. But it didn’t really lift.\n\nSo… about a year and eleven months now, I think. Almost two years. It feels strange saying that out loud, because I kept telling myself it was temporary, just a bad season, but it’s been going on that whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things, even if it's just a little, or has the pleasure completely gone from activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: It feels like the pleasure is pretty much gone. I keep trying to check, like maybe there’s still a little bit there and I’m just not noticing it, but… no. My art classes used to feel important to me, like even if I was having a rough week, I’d still get something out of showing up and making something. I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.\n\nMost of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. But if you mean this uncomfortable, unsettled feeling that’s just there sometimes, then maybe. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. It’s been… really noticeable, actually, but not in one consistent direction, which is part of why it feels hard to explain.

Some days I’m up and pacing like I can’t settle into my own body. I’ll go from the kitchen to the window, then back to the kitchen, then stand there and forget why I’m there, then go back to the window again. I’ll do that over and over until my legs actually ache, and even then I don’t always stop right away. It’s not like I’m exercising or trying to be productive. It’s more like I’m just… agitated, or wound up, but with nowhere for it to go.

And then other times it’s the complete opposite. I move so slowly it feels ridiculous. Like even making coffee feels like lifting wet cement. I’ll be standing there trying to get the mug, scoop the coffee, pour the water, and every little step feels like it takes all this effort. My arms feel heavy, my body feels heavy, and I know from the outside I must look like I’m moving in slow motion.

So yes, compared to before, I’m either pacing until I’m exhausted or I’m barely moving through basic things. It doesn’t feel like my normal pace at all.

My art classes used to matter to me a lot, and I’d go even when I was tired because it felt like mine, you know? And board-game nights with people, hiking, even thinking about getting back to the soccer team… I used to have these little threads pulling me out of the house.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my room and let everything slip away. I know I’m missing things, and I know I’m supposed to want to fix it or at least be upset enough to do something, but it’s like there’s no spark there. I’m just kind of watching my life shrink, and I can’t make myself care the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. It started not long after my divorce, like shortly after everything was finalized and I was trying to pretend I could just restart my life. At first I thought it was just the normal fallout, you know, like of course I’d feel empty for a while. But it didn’t really lift.\n\nSo… about a year and eleven months now, I think. Almost two years. It feels strange saying that out loud, because I kept telling myself it was temporary, just a bad season, but it’s been going on that whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things, even if it's just a little, or has the pleasure completely gone from activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: It feels like the pleasure is pretty much gone. I keep trying to check, like maybe there’s still a little bit there and I’m just not noticing it, but… no. My art classes used to feel important to me, like even if I was having a rough week, I’d still get something out of showing up and making something. Now I just think about going and it feels flat, like a chore I don’t even have the energy to pretend to care about.\n\nSame with board-game nights. I used to like the planning, the jokes, the whole routine of it. Hiking too—I used to feel clearer after being outside, even just a little. And soccer, I kept telling myself maybe I’d get back into it, but even that idea doesn’t pull at me anymore. I mostly just end up sitting in my room, letting those things pass by, and I know they used to matter, but I can’t feel that they matter now. It’s like the part of me that enjoyed them just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.\n\nMost of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. Maybe little blips where I was distracted for a few hours, but nothing that felt like I was back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you're doing something by yourself, like reading or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can put a movie on, or open a book, but it’s like I’m just… occupying my eyes. I don’t feel pulled into it the way I used to. I’ll sit there and realize I’ve watched twenty minutes and I couldn’t even tell you what happened, and it doesn’t bother me enough to rewind. I just let it keep going because at least the room isn’t completely silent.\n\nIt’s the same feeling as with everything else. I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not board-game nights, not hiking, not even the idea of getting back to the soccer team. Those things used to be parts of my life, like proof that I had interests and people and reasons to leave the house. Now I mostly just sit in my room while everything slips away, and even when I try to do something by myself, there’s no pleasure in it. It’s just time passing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know what “worried” means in a normal way, like thinking about bills or appointments or whether I forgot something, but when you ask if I’m anxious often, I kind of… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it as a separate thing.\n\nSometimes I feel uneasy, but I’m not always sure if that’s worry or just me feeling bad in general. It’s hard to separate it out. If you mean do I sit there with a clear list of things I’m worried about and feel nervous about them all day, I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m making some clear plan in my head, but I keep having these thoughts like… I just don’t want to wake up anymore. Like I really couldn’t see how I was supposed to keep doing another day and another day like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t know the exact number because I haven’t really been weighing myself regularly, but I can tell it’s changed. My jeans are really loose now — like not just “a little baggy,” but loose enough that I’ve had to knot the waistband to keep them from sliding around. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll notice it when I’m getting dressed and then just… not do anything about it.\n\nAnd eating has been strange. Most days I’ll go almost the whole day without eating, not because I’m trying to lose weight or anything like that, but because food just doesn’t have any appeal. It tastes like cardboard, or like I’m chewing just to get through it. I’ll think, “I should probably eat something,” and then hours go by and I still haven’t. So yeah, I think I’ve lost weight, probably a significant amount, but I don’t have a clean number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active you are or how you move compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s been… really noticeable, actually, but not in one consistent direction, which is part of why it feels hard to explain.\n\nSome days I’m up and pacing like I can’t settle into my own body. I’ll go from the kitchen to the window, then back to the kitchen, then stand there and forget why I’m there, then go back to the window again. I’ll do that over and over until my legs actually ache, and even then I don’t always stop right away. It’s not like I’m exercising or trying to be productive. It’s more like I’m just… agitated, or wound up, but with nowhere for it to go.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the complete opposite. I move so slowly it feels ridiculous. Like even making coffee feels like lifting wet cement. I’ll be standing there trying to get the mug, scoop the coffee, pour the water, and every little step feels like it takes all this effort. My arms feel heavy, my body feels heavy, and I know from the outside I must look like I’m moving in slow motion.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’m either pacing until I’m exhausted or I’m barely moving through basic things. It doesn’t feel like my normal pace at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know if I’d call it a clean pattern, like “every three weeks” or anything organized like that. It’s more like… since this really took over, the worst feeling is most of the time. Like the baseline became bad, if that makes sense.

There are moments where I’m a little less overwhelmed, but even then I don’t feel like myself. It’s not like I have these clear good periods where I’m interested in things again or excited or thinking, “Okay, I can build my life back.” Most of the time I feel deeply depressed, like everything has gone flat and heavy, and I barely care about anything I used to care about. Even things that should feel comforting just feel far away from me.

And I keep having this feeling — it’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like my life is already over, but my body is still here doing the motions. So when you ask about cycles, I guess I’d say there are bad days and worse days, and maybe a few hours here and there where it eases up slightly, but I don’t really get much of a break from that depressed, empty feeling.

And soccer, I kept telling myself maybe I’d get back into it, but even that idea doesn’t pull at me anymore. I mostly just end up sitting in my room, letting those things pass by, and I know they used to matter, but I can’t feel that they matter now. It’s like the part of me that enjoyed them just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.\n\nMost of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. Maybe little blips where I was distracted for a few hours, but nothing that felt like I was back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you're doing something by yourself, like reading or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can put a movie on, or open a book, but it’s like I’m just… occupying my eyes. I don’t feel pulled into it the way I used to. I’ll sit there and realize I’ve watched twenty minutes and I couldn’t even tell you what happened, and it doesn’t bother me enough to rewind. I just let it keep going because at least the room isn’t completely silent.\n\nIt’s the same feeling as with everything else. I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not board-game nights, not hiking, not even the idea of getting back to the soccer team. Those things used to be parts of my life, like proof that I had interests and people and reasons to leave the house. Now I mostly just sit in my room while everything slips away, and even when I try to do something by myself, there’s no pleasure in it. It’s just time passing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know what “worried” means in a normal way, like thinking about bills or appointments or whether I forgot something, but when you ask if I’m anxious often, I kind of… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it as a separate thing.\n\nSometimes I feel uneasy, but I’m not always sure if that’s worry or just me feeling bad in general. It’s hard to separate it out. If you mean do I sit there with a clear list of things I’m worried about and feel nervous about them all day, I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. But if you mean this uncomfortable, unsettled feeling that’s just there sometimes, then maybe. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive during this time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m making some clear plan in my head, but I keep having these thoughts like… I just don’t want to wake up anymore. Like if I could go to sleep and just not have to come back into this feeling, that would be a relief. It sounds awful when I say it plainly, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nAnd there was a time my friend found me, and I guess I had been saying it out loud — that I couldn’t keep living like this. I remember feeling embarrassed afterward, because it felt dramatic or like I was scaring people, but in the moment it didn’t feel dramatic. It felt true. Like I really couldn’t see how I was supposed to keep doing another day and another day like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t know the exact number because I haven’t really been weighing myself regularly, but I can tell it’s changed. My jeans are really loose now — like not just “a little baggy,” but loose enough that I’ve had to knot the waistband to keep them from sliding around. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll notice it when I’m getting dressed and then just… not do anything about it.\n\nAnd eating has been strange. Most days I’ll go almost the whole day without eating, not because I’m trying to lose weight or anything like that, but because food just doesn’t have any appeal. It tastes like cardboard, or like I’m chewing just to get through it. I’ll think, “I should probably eat something,” and then hours go by and I still haven’t. So yeah, I think I’ve lost weight, probably a significant amount, but I don’t have a clean number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active you are or how you move compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s been… really noticeable, actually, but not in one consistent direction, which is part of why it feels hard to explain.\n\nSome days I’m up and pacing like I can’t settle into my own body. I’ll go from the kitchen to the window, then back to the kitchen, then stand there and forget why I’m there, then go back to the window again. I’ll do that over and over until my legs actually ache, and even then I don’t always stop right away. It’s not like I’m exercising or trying to be productive. It’s more like I’m just… agitated, or wound up, but with nowhere for it to go.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the complete opposite. I move so slowly it feels ridiculous. Like even making coffee feels like lifting wet cement. I’ll be standing there trying to get the mug, scoop the coffee, pour the water, and every little step feels like it takes all this effort. My arms feel heavy, my body feels heavy, and I know from the outside I must look like I’m moving in slow motion.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’m either pacing until I’m exhausted or I’m barely moving through basic things. It doesn’t feel like my normal pace at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or cycles to when you feel your worst or slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a clean pattern, like “every three weeks” or anything organized like that. It’s more like… since this really took over, the worst feeling is most of the time. Like the baseline became bad, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are moments where I’m a little less overwhelmed, but even then I don’t feel like myself. It’s not like I have these clear good periods where I’m interested in things again or excited or thinking, “Okay, I can build my life back.” Most of the time I feel deeply depressed, like everything has gone flat and heavy, and I barely care about anything I used to care about. Even things that should feel comforting just feel far away from me.\n\nAnd I keep having this feeling — it’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like my life is already over, but my body is still here doing the motions. So when you ask about cycles, I guess I’d say there are bad days and worse days, and maybe a few hours here and there where it eases up slightly, but I don’t really get much of a break from that depressed, empty feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F32.2", "F32.3", "F33.2", "F33.3"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t know the exact number because I haven’t really been weighing myself regularly, but I can tell it’s changed. My jeans are really loose now — like not just “a little baggy,” but loose enough that I’ve had to knot the waistband to keep them from sliding around. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll notice it when I’m getting dressed and then just… not do anything about it.\n\nAnd eating has been strange. Most days I’ll go almost the whole day without eating, not because I’m trying to lose weight or anything like that, but because food just doesn’t have any appeal. It tastes like cardboard, or like I’m chewing just to get through it. I’ll think, “I should probably eat something,” and then hours go by and I still haven’t. So yeah, I think I’ve lost weight, probably a significant amount, but I don’t have a clean number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active you are or how you move compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s been… really noticeable, actually, but not in one consistent direction, which is part of why it feels hard to explain.\n\nSome days I’m up and pacing like I can’t settle into my own body. I’ll go from the kitchen to the window, then back to the kitchen, then stand there and forget why I’m there, then go back to the window again. I’ll do that over and over until my legs actually ache, and even then I don’t always stop right away. It’s not like I’m exercising or trying to be productive. It’s more like I’m just… agitated, or wound up, but with nowhere for it to go.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the complete opposite. I move so slowly it feels ridiculous. Like even making coffee feels like lifting wet cement. I’ll be standing there trying to get the mug, scoop the coffee, pour the water, and every little step feels like it takes all this effort. My arms feel heavy, my body feels heavy, and I know from the outside I must look like I’m moving in slow motion.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’m either pacing until I’m exhausted or I’m barely moving through basic things. It doesn’t feel like my normal pace at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or cycles to when you feel your worst or slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a clean pattern, like “every three weeks” or anything organized like that. It’s more like… since this really took over, the worst feeling is most of the time. Like the baseline became bad, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are moments where I’m a little less overwhelmed, but even then I don’t feel like myself. It’s not like I have these clear good periods where I’m interested in things again or excited or thinking, “Okay, I can build my life back.” Most of the time I feel deeply depressed, like everything has gone flat and heavy, and I barely care about anything I used to care about. Even things that should feel comforting just feel far away from me.\n\nAnd I keep having this feeling — it’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like my life is already over, but my body is still here doing the motions. So when you ask about cycles, I guess I’d say there are bad days and worse days, and maybe a few hours here and there where it eases up slightly, but I don’t really get much of a break from that depressed, empty feeling.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had more than one episode of feeling this depressed, or has it been a continuous feeling over the past two years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressive Episode Duration\nmanifestation: \"The depression became constant around the following summer, and it has lasted about 1 year and 4 months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been more continuous, I think. There were times before where I’d get low and then sort of come back up, at least enough to function, but this… this became constant around the following summer. Like that’s when it stopped feeling like a rough patch and started feeling like the weather had changed permanently.

So from then to now, it’s been about a year and four months of feeling depressed pretty much all the time. Not every single minute is exactly the same intensity, but I don’t feel like I’ve had a real break from it. It’s more like it settled in and stayed.

I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.\n\nMost of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. Maybe little blips where I was distracted for a few hours, but nothing that felt like I was back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you're doing something by yourself, like reading or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Not really. But if you mean this uncomfortable, unsettled feeling that’s just there sometimes, then maybe. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m making some clear plan in my head, but I keep having these thoughts like… I just don’t want to wake up anymore. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I’ve tried to pay attention to that, because I know people ask about mornings, but I can’t honestly say there’s a clear morning pattern.

Some mornings are awful, but then some afternoons are awful too, and evenings can be just as bad. It’s more like I wake up and check what kind of bad it is that day, if that makes sense. Sometimes the morning feels heavier because I’m facing the whole day ahead of me, but I don’t know if that means the depression is actually worse in the morning or if it’s just the thought of having to get through another day.

So… I haven’t noticed a reliable “mornings are the worst” thing. It’s just kind of there most of the time.

I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.\n\nMost of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. Maybe little blips where I was distracted for a few hours, but nothing that felt like I was back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you're doing something by yourself, like reading or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can put a movie on, or open a book, but it’s like I’m just… occupying my eyes. I don’t feel pulled into it the way I used to. I’ll sit there and realize I’ve watched twenty minutes and I couldn’t even tell you what happened, and it doesn’t bother me enough to rewind. I just let it keep going because at least the room isn’t completely silent.\n\nIt’s the same feeling as with everything else. I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not board-game nights, not hiking, not even the idea of getting back to the soccer team. Now I mostly just sit in my room while everything slips away, and even when I try to do something by myself, there’s no pleasure in it. I mean, I know what “worried” means in a normal way, like thinking about bills or appointments or whether I forgot something, but when you ask if I’m anxious often, I kind of… I don’t know. But if you mean this uncomfortable, unsettled feeling that’s just there sometimes, then maybe. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive during this time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m making some clear plan in my head, but I keep having these thoughts like… I just don’t want to wake up anymore. Like if I could go to sleep and just not have to come back into this feeling, that would be a relief. My jeans are really loose now — like not just “a little baggy,” but loose enough that I’ve had to knot the waistband to keep them from sliding around. Most days I’ll go almost the whole day without eating, not because I’m trying to lose weight or anything like that, but because food just doesn’t have any appeal. My art classes used to matter to me a lot, and I’d go even when I was tired because it felt like mine, you know? And board-game nights with people, hiking, even thinking about getting back to the soccer team… I used to have these little threads pulling me out of the house.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my room and let everything slip away. I know I’m missing things, and I know I’m supposed to want to fix it or at least be upset enough to do something, but it’s like there’s no spark there. I’m just kind of watching my life shrink, and I can’t make myself care the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time. It started not long after my divorce, like shortly after everything was finalized and I was trying to pretend I could just restart my life. At first I thought it was just the normal fallout, you know, like of course I’d feel empty for a while. But it didn’t really lift.\n\nSo… about a year and eleven months now, I think. Almost two years. It feels strange saying that out loud, because I kept telling myself it was temporary, just a bad season, but it’s been going on that whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things, even if it's just a little, or has the pleasure completely gone from activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: It feels like the pleasure is pretty much gone. I keep trying to check, like maybe there’s still a little bit there and I’m just not noticing it, but… no. My art classes used to feel important to me, like even if I was having a rough week, I’d still get something out of showing up and making something. Now I just think about going and it feels flat, like a chore I don’t even have the energy to pretend to care about.\n\nSame with board-game nights. I used to like the planning, the jokes, the whole routine of it. Hiking too—I used to feel clearer after being outside, even just a little. And soccer, I kept telling myself maybe I’d get back into it, but even that idea doesn’t pull at me anymore. I mostly just end up sitting in my room, letting those things pass by, and I know they used to matter, but I can’t feel that they matter now. It’s like the part of me that enjoyed them just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.\n\nMost of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. Maybe little blips where I was distracted for a few hours, but nothing that felt like I was back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you're doing something by yourself, like reading or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can put a movie on, or open a book, but it’s like I’m just… occupying my eyes. I don’t feel pulled into it the way I used to. I’ll sit there and realize I’ve watched twenty minutes and I couldn’t even tell you what happened, and it doesn’t bother me enough to rewind. I just let it keep going because at least the room isn’t completely silent.\n\nIt’s the same feeling as with everything else. I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not board-game nights, not hiking, not even the idea of getting back to the soccer team. Those things used to be parts of my life, like proof that I had interests and people and reasons to leave the house. Now I mostly just sit in my room while everything slips away, and even when I try to do something by myself, there’s no pleasure in it. It’s just time passing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know what “worried” means in a normal way, like thinking about bills or appointments or whether I forgot something, but when you ask if I’m anxious often, I kind of… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it as a separate thing.\n\nSometimes I feel uneasy, but I’m not always sure if that’s worry or just me feeling bad in general. It’s hard to separate it out. If you mean do I sit there with a clear list of things I’m worried about and feel nervous about them all day, I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. But if you mean this uncomfortable, unsettled feeling that’s just there sometimes, then maybe. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive during this time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m making some clear plan in my head, but I keep having these thoughts like… I just don’t want to wake up anymore. Like if I could go to sleep and just not have to come back into this feeling, that would be a relief. It sounds awful when I say it plainly, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nAnd there was a time my friend found me, and I guess I had been saying it out loud — that I couldn’t keep living like this. I remember feeling embarrassed afterward, because it felt dramatic or like I was scaring people, but in the moment it didn’t feel dramatic. It felt true. Like I really couldn’t see how I was supposed to keep doing another day and another day like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t know the exact number because I haven’t really been weighing myself regularly, but I can tell it’s changed. My jeans are really loose now — like not just “a little baggy,” but loose enough that I’ve had to knot the waistband to keep them from sliding around. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll notice it when I’m getting dressed and then just… not do anything about it.\n\nAnd eating has been strange. Most days I’ll go almost the whole day without eating, not because I’m trying to lose weight or anything like that, but because food just doesn’t have any appeal. It tastes like cardboard, or like I’m chewing just to get through it. I’ll think, “I should probably eat something,” and then hours go by and I still haven’t. So yeah, I think I’ve lost weight, probably a significant amount, but I don’t have a clean number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active you are or how you move compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s been… really noticeable, actually, but not in one consistent direction, which is part of why it feels hard to explain.\n\nSome days I’m up and pacing like I can’t settle into my own body. I’ll go from the kitchen to the window, then back to the kitchen, then stand there and forget why I’m there, then go back to the window again. I’ll do that over and over until my legs actually ache, and even then I don’t always stop right away. It’s not like I’m exercising or trying to be productive. It’s more like I’m just… agitated, or wound up, but with nowhere for it to go.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the complete opposite. I move so slowly it feels ridiculous. Like even making coffee feels like lifting wet cement. I’ll be standing there trying to get the mug, scoop the coffee, pour the water, and every little step feels like it takes all this effort. My arms feel heavy, my body feels heavy, and I know from the outside I must look like I’m moving in slow motion.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’m either pacing until I’m exhausted or I’m barely moving through basic things. It doesn’t feel like my normal pace at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or cycles to when you feel your worst or slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a clean pattern, like “every three weeks” or anything organized like that. It’s more like… since this really took over, the worst feeling is most of the time. Like the baseline became bad, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are moments where I’m a little less overwhelmed, but even then I don’t feel like myself. It’s not like I have these clear good periods where I’m interested in things again or excited or thinking, “Okay, I can build my life back.” Most of the time I feel deeply depressed, like everything has gone flat and heavy, and I barely care about anything I used to care about. Even things that should feel comforting just feel far away from me.\n\nAnd I keep having this feeling — it’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like my life is already over, but my body is still here doing the motions. So when you ask about cycles, I guess I’d say there are bad days and worse days, and maybe a few hours here and there where it eases up slightly, but I don’t really get much of a break from that depressed, empty feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this depressed, or has it been a continuous feeling over the past two years?\n\nPatient: It’s been more continuous, I think. There were times before where I’d get low and then sort of come back up, at least enough to function, but this… this became constant around the following summer. Like that’s when it stopped feeling like a rough patch and started feeling like the weather had changed permanently.\n\nSo from then to now, it’s been about a year and four months of feeling depressed pretty much all the time. Not every single minute is exactly the same intensity, but I don’t feel like I’ve had a real break from it. It’s more like it settled in and stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worse in the morning?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve tried to pay attention to that, because I know people ask about mornings, but I can’t honestly say there’s a clear morning pattern.\n\nSome mornings are awful, but then some afternoons are awful too, and evenings can be just as bad. It’s more like I wake up and check what kind of bad it is that day, if that makes sense. Sometimes the morning feels heavier because I’m facing the whole day ahead of me, but I don’t know if that means the depression is actually worse in the morning or if it’s just the thought of having to get through another day.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a reliable “mornings are the worst” thing. It’s just kind of there most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s probably one of the biggest reasons I’m here, honestly. It’s not just that I’m sad or in a bad mood. It’s like the part of me that used to respond to good things is just… not working.

I used to care about things. I took art classes, and even when I wasn’t great at something, I liked being there, messing with colors, learning techniques, talking to people a little. I used to go to board-game nights and actually look forward to it, even if I was tired. Hiking used to make me feel clear-headed, like I could breathe again. And I kept thinking I should get back to the soccer team, because that used to feel like part of who I was.

But now, even if something good happens, or someone invites me, or I know logically that it should be enjoyable, I just don’t feel it. I mostly sit in my room and watch everything slip away. People will say, “Come on, you’ll feel better if you get out,” and I understand why they say that, but when I try to imagine doing those things, there’s just nothing there. No pull toward it. No spark. Sometimes I’ll agree to something and then cancel because it feels impossible, but even when I do show up, it’s like I’m behind glass.

So yes, I think I’m unable to really experience pleasure. Or if I can, it’s so faint and brief that it doesn’t feel like pleasure the way it used to. It’s more like I can recognize that something is supposed to be good, but I can’t actually enjoy it.

At first I thought it was just the normal fallout, you know, like of course I’d feel empty for a while. But it didn’t really lift.\n\nSo… about a year and eleven months now, I think. Almost two years. It feels strange saying that out loud, because I kept telling myself it was temporary, just a bad season, but it’s been going on that whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things, even if it's just a little, or has the pleasure completely gone from activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: It feels like the pleasure is pretty much gone. I keep trying to check, like maybe there’s still a little bit there and I’m just not noticing it, but… no. My art classes used to feel important to me, like even if I was having a rough week, I’d still get something out of showing up and making something. Now I just think about going and it feels flat, like a chore I don’t even have the energy to pretend to care about.\n\nSame with board-game nights. I used to like the planning, the jokes, the whole routine of it. Hiking too—I used to feel clearer after being outside, even just a little. And soccer, I kept telling myself maybe I’d get back into it, but even that idea doesn’t pull at me anymore. I mostly just end up sitting in my room, letting those things pass by, and I know they used to matter, but I can’t feel that they matter now. It’s like the part of me that enjoyed them just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two years where you felt better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’ve had moments where I can function a little better, or I can get through a day without crying or without just lying there staring at the wall, but I wouldn’t call it feeling like myself.\n\nMost of the time since this took over, it’s been this heavy depressed feeling, like I’m not just scared or confused or having a bad week. It’s more like everything in me has gone dim. I’m barely interested in anything, even things I know used to matter to me, and there’s this awful sense that my life is already over somehow, even though I’m still here and still supposed to be living it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, a real stretch where I felt normal again—where I cared about my life, or felt connected to things, or could imagine a future and believe in it—no. I don’t think I’ve had that. Maybe little blips where I was distracted for a few hours, but nothing that felt like I was back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you're doing something by yourself, like reading or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can put a movie on, or open a book, but it’s like I’m just… occupying my eyes. I don’t feel pulled into it the way I used to. I’ll sit there and realize I’ve watched twenty minutes and I couldn’t even tell you what happened, and it doesn’t bother me enough to rewind. I just let it keep going because at least the room isn’t completely silent.\n\nIt’s the same feeling as with everything else. I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not board-game nights, not hiking, not even the idea of getting back to the soccer team. Those things used to be parts of my life, like proof that I had interests and people and reasons to leave the house. Now I mostly just sit in my room while everything slips away, and even when I try to do something by myself, there’s no pleasure in it. It’s just time passing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know what “worried” means in a normal way, like thinking about bills or appointments or whether I forgot something, but when you ask if I’m anxious often, I kind of… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it as a separate thing.\n\nSometimes I feel uneasy, but I’m not always sure if that’s worry or just me feeling bad in general. It’s hard to separate it out. If you mean do I sit there with a clear list of things I’m worried about and feel nervous about them all day, I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. But if you mean this uncomfortable, unsettled feeling that’s just there sometimes, then maybe. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive during this time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m making some clear plan in my head, but I keep having these thoughts like… I just don’t want to wake up anymore. Like if I could go to sleep and just not have to come back into this feeling, that would be a relief. It sounds awful when I say it plainly, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nAnd there was a time my friend found me, and I guess I had been saying it out loud — that I couldn’t keep living like this. I remember feeling embarrassed afterward, because it felt dramatic or like I was scaring people, but in the moment it didn’t feel dramatic. It felt true. Like I really couldn’t see how I was supposed to keep doing another day and another day like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t know the exact number because I haven’t really been weighing myself regularly, but I can tell it’s changed. My jeans are really loose now — like not just “a little baggy,” but loose enough that I’ve had to knot the waistband to keep them from sliding around. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll notice it when I’m getting dressed and then just… not do anything about it.\n\nAnd eating has been strange. Most days I’ll go almost the whole day without eating, not because I’m trying to lose weight or anything like that, but because food just doesn’t have any appeal. It tastes like cardboard, or like I’m chewing just to get through it. I’ll think, “I should probably eat something,” and then hours go by and I still haven’t. So yeah, I think I’ve lost weight, probably a significant amount, but I don’t have a clean number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active you are or how you move compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s been… really noticeable, actually, but not in one consistent direction, which is part of why it feels hard to explain.\n\nSome days I’m up and pacing like I can’t settle into my own body. I’ll go from the kitchen to the window, then back to the kitchen, then stand there and forget why I’m there, then go back to the window again. I’ll do that over and over until my legs actually ache, and even then I don’t always stop right away. It’s not like I’m exercising or trying to be productive. It’s more like I’m just… agitated, or wound up, but with nowhere for it to go.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the complete opposite. I move so slowly it feels ridiculous. Like even making coffee feels like lifting wet cement. I’ll be standing there trying to get the mug, scoop the coffee, pour the water, and every little step feels like it takes all this effort. My arms feel heavy, my body feels heavy, and I know from the outside I must look like I’m moving in slow motion.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’m either pacing until I’m exhausted or I’m barely moving through basic things. It doesn’t feel like my normal pace at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or cycles to when you feel your worst or slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a clean pattern, like “every three weeks” or anything organized like that. It’s more like… since this really took over, the worst feeling is most of the time. Like the baseline became bad, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are moments where I’m a little less overwhelmed, but even then I don’t feel like myself. It’s not like I have these clear good periods where I’m interested in things again or excited or thinking, “Okay, I can build my life back.” Most of the time I feel deeply depressed, like everything has gone flat and heavy, and I barely care about anything I used to care about. Even things that should feel comforting just feel far away from me.\n\nAnd I keep having this feeling — it’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic — like my life is already over, but my body is still here doing the motions. So when you ask about cycles, I guess I’d say there are bad days and worse days, and maybe a few hours here and there where it eases up slightly, but I don’t really get much of a break from that depressed, empty feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this depressed, or has it been a continuous feeling over the past two years?\n\nPatient: It’s been more continuous, I think. There were times before where I’d get low and then sort of come back up, at least enough to function, but this… this became constant around the following summer. Like that’s when it stopped feeling like a rough patch and started feeling like the weather had changed permanently.\n\nSo from then to now, it’s been about a year and four months of feeling depressed pretty much all the time. Not every single minute is exactly the same intensity, but I don’t feel like I’ve had a real break from it. It’s more like it settled in and stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worse in the morning?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve tried to pay attention to that, because I know people ask about mornings, but I can’t honestly say there’s a clear morning pattern.\n\nSome mornings are awful, but then some afternoons are awful too, and evenings can be just as bad. It’s more like I wake up and check what kind of bad it is that day, if that makes sense. Sometimes the morning feels heavier because I’m facing the whole day ahead of me, but I don’t know if that means the depression is actually worse in the morning or if it’s just the thought of having to get through another day.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a reliable “mornings are the worst” thing. It’s just kind of there most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure even when good things happen to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably one of the biggest reasons I’m here, honestly. It’s not just that I’m sad or in a bad mood. It’s like the part of me that used to respond to good things is just… not working.\n\nI used to care about things. I took art classes, and even when I wasn’t great at something, I liked being there, messing with colors, learning techniques, talking to people a little. I used to go to board-game nights and actually look forward to it, even if I was tired. Hiking used to make me feel clear-headed, like I could breathe again. And I kept thinking I should get back to the soccer team, because that used to feel like part of who I was.\n\nBut now, even if something good happens, or someone invites me, or I know logically that it should be enjoyable, I just don’t feel it. I mostly sit in my room and watch everything slip away. People will say, “Come on, you’ll feel better if you get out,” and I understand why they say that, but when I try to imagine doing those things, there’s just nothing there. No pull toward it. No spark. Sometimes I’ll agree to something and then cancel because it feels impossible, but even when I do show up, it’s like I’m behind glass.\n\nSo yes, I think I’m unable to really experience pleasure. Or if I can, it’s so faint and brief that it doesn’t feel like pleasure the way it used to. It’s more like I can recognize that something is supposed to be good, but I can’t actually enjoy it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Most of the time since this illness took over, I haven’t just been scared or confused—I’ve been deeply depressed, barely interested in anything, and feeling like my life is already over.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch before the worst depression set in when I could still cook sometimes and answer friends, but I was already hearing the voice and believing the hallway lights were signals about me for weeks.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve stopped taking freelance art-education work, missed community events and soccer, and I need my friend checking on me because I’m not reliably eating, showering, or keeping myself safe.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since shortly after the divorce, about 1 year and 11 months now.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the beliefs about being watched started a few months after the divorce and have been going on for about 1 year and 8 months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The depression became constant around the following summer, and it has lasted about 1 year and 4 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this depressed, or has it been a continuous feeling over the past two years?\n\nPatient: It’s been more continuous, I think. There were times before where I’d get low and then sort of come back up, at least enough to function, but this… this became constant around the following summer. Like that’s when it stopped feeling like a rough patch and started feeling like the weather had changed permanently.\n\nSo from then to now, it’s been about a year and four months of feeling depressed pretty much all the time. Not every single minute is exactly the same intensity, but I don’t feel like I’ve had a real break from it. It’s more like it settled in and stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worse in the morning?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve tried to pay attention to that, because I know people ask about mornings, but I can’t honestly say there’s a clear morning pattern.\n\nSome mornings are awful, but then some afternoons are awful too, and evenings can be just as bad. It’s more like I wake up and check what kind of bad it is that day, if that makes sense. Sometimes the morning feels heavier because I’m facing the whole day ahead of me, but I don’t know if that means the depression is actually worse in the morning or if it’s just the thought of having to get through another day.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a reliable “mornings are the worst” thing. It’s just kind of there most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure even when good things happen to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably one of the biggest reasons I’m here, honestly. It’s not just that I’m sad or in a bad mood. It’s like the part of me that used to respond to good things is just… not working.\n\nI used to care about things. I took art classes, and even when I wasn’t great at something, I liked being there, messing with colors, learning techniques, talking to people a little. I used to go to board-game nights and actually look forward to it, even if I was tired. Hiking used to make me feel clear-headed, like I could breathe again. And I kept thinking I should get back to the soccer team, because that used to feel like part of who I was.\n\nBut now, even if something good happens, or someone invites me, or I know logically that it should be enjoyable, I just don’t feel it. I mostly sit in my room and watch everything slip away. People will say, “Come on, you’ll feel better if you get out,” and I understand why they say that, but when I try to imagine doing those things, there’s just nothing there. No pull toward it. No spark. Sometimes I’ll agree to something and then cancel because it feels impossible, but even when I do show up, it’s like I’m behind glass.\n\nSo yes, I think I’m unable to really experience pleasure. Or if I can, it’s so faint and brief that it doesn’t feel like pleasure the way it used to. It’s more like I can recognize that something is supposed to be good, but I can’t actually enjoy it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your low mood or depression might be related to a specific time of year?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. I’ve tried to think about that, because it would make it easier in a way if I could point to winter or summer or some pattern like, “Oh, this happens when the seasons change.” But I haven’t noticed anything that clear.

It’s not like I can say I always get worse when it gets dark earlier, or that I reliably improve when it’s sunny out. Sometimes a gray day feels like it matches how I feel, but then I’ve also felt just as bad on bright days, which almost makes me feel more disconnected, because everyone else acts like the weather should help.

So… I’m not sure it’s tied to a specific time of year. It feels more constant than seasonal, at least from what I can tell.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I don’t know, I just don’t care about anything anymore. Like, things I used to actually look forward to don’t feel like anything now.
